\mathcal{W}^{4,0}_{nv}=\Lambda^{2}L\text{ and }P^{2}_{nv}=l\wedge l^{\perp}.

In particular, note that the limit line does not determine the limit plane in this case.
In terms of the coordinates for
R_{B}
, it follows that

\gamma(t)\text{ limits to }(0:b_{0}:c_{0}:d_{0}:0)\text{ where }c_{0}^{2}-b_{0}d_{0}=0.

The reasons is that the MUM Lagrangian will be preserved by
R_{B}
, for instance because the fixed curve of the reflection on the base will still have the same naive limit, so it must be that for the coordinates of
\mathcal{W}^{4,0}_{nv}
, either the middle three, or the outer two, vanish.
However, the action of
R_{B}
changes the orientation of the MUM Lagrangian
L
, since the composition with another reflection should yield a nontrivial unipotent transformation inside
L
, so it must be the case that the outer two coordinates of
L
vanish.

On the other hand, the limit of

(a:0:0:0:e)\wedge(0:b:c:d:0)\text{ is }(a_{0}:0:0:0:e_{0})\wedge(0:b_{0}:c_{0}:d_{0}:0)

where
a_{0}e_{0}=0
and
a_{0}+e_{0}=1
, and where
(b_{0}:c_{0}:d_{0})
is the same as the coordinates of
L
.
This isotropic
2
-plane corresponds to the photon
\phi(l)
determined by the line
d_{0}f_{1}+c_{0}f_{2}
(or equivalently
c_{0}f_{1}+b_{0}f_{2}
, if
d_{0}=0
and using the defining relation).

3.3.14.
Fundamental polyhedron

We can now put together the information from the analysis of the period map on the each of the segments on the real axis, together with
Theorem 2.4.7
.
Specifically, let
X
be the orbifold associated to one of the good hypergeometric parameters provided by
Theorem 3.1.6
.
It has an open set identified with
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})\setminus\{0,1\infty\}
, and possibly orbifold points of orders
M
or
N
at
0
, or
\infty
.
Let
\overline{X}
denote the completed orbifold, so
\overline{X}\setminus X=:S
consists of
1
, and possibly
0
or
\infty
.
We can decompose
X
into cells:

X=X^{+}\coprod X^{-}\coprod X^{r}\coprod X^{orb}

where
X^{\pm}
correspond to the upper/lower half planes,
X^{r}
corresponds to the part on the real axis and itself decomposes into three open segments
X^{r}_{A},X^{r}_{B},X^{r}_{C}
according to the three reflections from
§3.2.5
, and
X^{orb}
corresponds to the orbifold points (there can be
0,1
or
2
such points).

Recall that
Theorem 2.4.7
provides a developing map
\Dev^{P,+}
from a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-bundle over
\widetilde{X}
to the pseudosphere
\mathbb{S}^{1,3}(W_{\mathbb{R}})
, equivariant for the action of the monodromy group
\Gamma
generated by the three involutions
R_{A},R_{B},R_{C}
.
A direct consequence of theorem
Theorem 2.4.7
and the analysis of the period map on the real axis is:

3.3.15 Theorem

(Fundamental polyhedron)
.

Define
\mathcal{P}^{+}
to be the closure in
\mathbb{S}^{1,3}(W_{\mathbb{R}})
of
\Dev^{P,+}(X^{+})
.
Then it has a cell decomposition, with

interior:

the image of
\Dev^{P,+}(X^{+})
, diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

codimension 1:

the image of
\Dev^{P,+}(X^{r})
, consisting of three faces diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

codimension 2:

the image of the finitely many orbifold points
X^{orb}
, each diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

Additionally, the closure of
\mathcal{P}^{+}
in
\mathbb{P}^{+}(W_{\mathbb{R}})
consists of finitely many photons, corresponding to the cusps, i.e. points in
\overline{X}\setminus X
.
The cusps are of two types, depending on the corresponding unipotent part of the monodromy matrix – a rank
1
unipotent (
§3.3.11
) or maximally unipotent monodromy (
§3.3.13
).

The polyhedron
\mathcal{P}^{+}
is a fundamental domain for the action of the group
\Gamma=\left\langle R_{A},R_{B},R_{C}\right\rangle
generated by the three reflections.
Each reflection fixes one of the codimension
1
faces as a set, and inside it has a fixed point locus of real dimension
1
.

3.3.16 Remark

(On diffeomorphism types)
.

By the discussion in
§3.3.8
, we have that
\mathbb{S}^{1,3}(W_{\mathbb{R}})
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
(where the second factor is better viewed as
\mathbb{H}^{3}
).
In fact, all the spaces appearing in
Theorem 3.3.15
have an
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-factor.
If we ignore it, and view the picture in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, then a cartoon structure of
\mathcal{P}^{+}
is as follows.
Its interior if foliated by a real
2
-parameter family of geodesics.
The codimension
1
faces are
1
-parameter families of geodesics, and the codimension
2
faces (coming from orbifold points) are single geodesics.
The faces that occur “at infinity” yield just points in the boundary (recall that we ignore the
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-factor).
In this sense, the picture is reminiscent of a quasi-Fuchsian surface in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, of small curvature, and its family of normal geodesics, except that the surface is missing and only the geodesics remain (see Epstein
[
Eps87
]
for more on the hyperbolic version).

Proof of
Theorem 3.3.15
.

By
Theorem 2.4.7
, the developing map
\Dev^{P,+}
is a diffeomorphism between a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-bundle over
\widetilde{X}
and
\mathbb{S}^{1,3}(W_{\mathbb{R}})
.
The assertions about the decompositions of
\mathcal{P}^{+}
and
\mathbb{S}^{1,3}
follow from the analogous ones for the decomposition of
\widetilde{X}
under the reflection group generated by the orientation-reversing hyperbolic isometries corresponding to the three open segments on the real axis.

It remains to analyze the behavior of a single reflection acting on the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-bundle over the real axis, since the reflection fixes pointwise the lift of the axis in the hyperbolic plane.
Recall that if

\gamma(t)=(a:\sqrt{-1}b:\sqrt{-1}c:\sqrt{-1}d:e)

say normalized to
ae=1=c^{2}-bd
, then the fiber over the corresponding point consists of real unit positive-definite vectors that are orthogonal to
\gamma(t)
(and hence to its complex-conjugate).
Of these, precisely one is fixed by the reflection, namely
(-a:0:0:0:e)
(see
Eqn. (3.3.2)
), so as
t
moves along the real axis, the fixed points sweep out a real
1
-dimensional submanifold.
∎

4.
Elements of Lie theory and Anosov representations

This section develops the necessary tools to study the global geometry of the monodromy of a VHS satisfying assumption A.
The relevant notion is that of Anosov representation, introduced by Labourie
[
Lab06
]
and studied further by Guichard–Wienhard
[
GW12
]
, as well as Kapovich–Leeb–Porti
[
KLP18
]
and many subsequent authors.

Our main examples of monodromy contain unipotents, so we need to extend the relevant concepts in Anosov representations allowing for this possibility.
We call the resulting representations “log-Anosov”, based on the logarithmic divergence of certain sequences associated to the cusp behavior, as well as in analogy with the algebro-geometric context where logarithmic poles and more general logarithmic structures make the situation at cusps well-behaved.

In a first draft of this text we developed some of the basic notions related to log-Anosov representations, but these are no longer necessary with the appearance of a treatment of this case by Zhu
[
Zhu19
]
and Kapovich–Leeb
[
KL18
]
, as well as Canary–Zhang–Zimmer
[
CZZ21
]
.
Let us also emphasize that one can take an intrinsic approach, looking at representations with target a semisimple group
G
, or an extrinsic approach where
G
is itself mapped via a linear representation.
Both points of view are useful, and it is particularly useful to pass between different representations.

To do so effectively, we introduce in
§4.2
(log)-dominated cocycles.
These generalize the notions introduced by Bochi–Potrie–Sambarino
[
BPS19
]
to allow for a general poset structure in the domination bundles.
One (of many) motivations is that the notion of domination in cocycles is not stable under the constructions of linear algebra (direct sum, tensor product, etc.) and one looses information in the standard definition each time such an operation is performed.

We introduce another convenient concept, that of a stable point, in analogy with the notion in Geometric Invariant Theory, in
§4.4
.
The analogy was proposed by Kapovich–Leeb–Porti
[
KLP18
]
, where it was used in the context of actions on flag manifolds.
We use it to give a self-contained treatment of the proper discontinuity results we need later.
It is also likely that the same tools will have broader applicability.

Two further new results on Anosov representations might be of interest.
First, we classify the integral points on the limit set, showing that they have to come from cusps (see
Theorem 4.5.3
).
Second, we show in
Theorem 4.6.3
that for an
\alpha_{1}
-log-Anosov representation containing a special kind of unipotent, the action on the limit set in the Lagrangian Grassmannian is minimal.
This is in contrast to Hitchin representations, where this is far from being true.

Finally, in
Appendix A
we address a useful technical point, which is not needed in the main text.
Namely, with the correct choice of metric in the cusp, the dynamics under the geodesic flow will exhibit the correct exponential behavior, as if the space were compact.
We introduce there the notion of log-proximal element (an analogue of proximal semisimple elements), the monodromy weight filtration, which controls the growth, and the notion of admissible metric which ensures the correct asymptotic behavior.

4.1.
Conventions and definitions

A concise summary of most of the structure theory of real Lie groups used below is contained in Knapp’s
[
Kna97
]
.

4.1.1.
Setup

Let
G
denote the real points of a real linear semisimple algebraic group, with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.
Pick a Cartan involution
\sigma\colon G\to G
, with fixed point set a maximal compact
K\subset G
.
Continue to denote the involution on the Lie algebra by
\sigma\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}
and let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{q}
denote the
\pm 1
-eigenspace decomposition, with
\mathfrak{k}=\Lie K
.

4.1.2.
Restricted root systems

Pick a maximal abelian subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathfrak{q}
.
The adjoint action of
\mathfrak{a}
on
\mathfrak{g}
gives a weight space decomposition

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{0}\oplus\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Phi}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}

where
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
is the centralizer of
\mathfrak{a}
and
\Phi\subset\mathfrak{a}^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}
are the
restricted roots
.
Note that
\mathfrak{g}_{0}=\mathfrak{a}\oplus(\mathfrak{g}_{0}\cap\mathfrak{k})
since nothing in
\mathfrak{q}
can commute with
\mathfrak{a}
.

Pick now a set of
positive roots

\Phi^{+}\subset\Phi
, i.e.
\Phi^{+}
is contained in a half-space and
\Phi=\Phi^{+}\coprod\Phi^{-}
(with
-\Phi^{+}=\Phi^{-}
) and let
\Delta\subset\Phi^{+}
denote the resulting set of
simple roots
; the elements of
\Delta
give a vector space basis for
\mathfrak{a}
.
The
positive Weyl chamber

\mathfrak{a}^{+}\subset\mathfrak{a}
is defined by

\mathfrak{a}^{+}=\left\{v\in\mathfrak{a}\colon\alpha(v)\geq 0,\,\forall\alpha\in\Delta\right\}

and the associated semigroup is denoted
A^{+}\subset G
.

Recall also that there is a Weyl-group invariant inner product, denoted
(x|y)
, on
\mathfrak{a}^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}
and
\mathfrak{a}
which we will use to identify them for some constructions.
For the fixed choice of simple roots
\Delta
, there are associated
fundamental weights

\left\{\omega_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha\in\Delta}\subset\mathfrak{a}^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}
defined by the relation

\frac{2}{(\beta|\beta)}(\beta|\omega_{\alpha})=\delta_{\beta\alpha}\quad\forall\beta,\alpha\in\Delta

Using the identification of
\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{a}^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}
via the invariant inner product, the fundamental weights can also be viewed as generators of the positive Weyl chamber.

4.1.3.
The centralizer of
\mathfrak{a}
in
K

Consider the subgroup of elements in
K
with trivial adjoint action on
\mathfrak{a}
.
It will be denoted
Z_{K}(\mathfrak{a})
, and it is more commonly referred to in Lie theory as
M\subset K
.
Its Lie algebra is
\mathfrak{g}_{0}\cap\mathfrak{k}
in the notation of
§4.1.2
.

4.1.4.
Parabolic subgroups

Fix a subset
\theta\subseteq\Delta
of simple roots.
Our convention in
§4.3.1
is that the singular values of the Anosov representation will diverge away from the union of the faces
\left(\ker\alpha\right)\cap\mathfrak{a}^{+}
for
\alpha\in\theta
.

Set
\theta^{c}:=\Delta\setminus\theta
.
Define
\Phi_{\theta^{c}}
to be the intersection of
\Phi
with the real span of the elements in
{\theta^{c}}
, and similarly the decomposition
\Phi_{\theta^{c}}=\Phi_{\theta^{c}}^{+}\coprod\Phi_{\theta^{c}}^{-}
.
With these conventions, define further:

•

The parabolic subalgebra:

\mathfrak{p}_{\theta}^{+}:=\mathfrak{g}_{0}\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Phi^{-}_{\theta^{c}}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}.

Note that it is
\Phi^{-}_{\theta^{c}}
that’s used.

•

The parabolic subgroup
P_{\theta}^{+}
with
\Lie P_{\theta}^{+}=\mathfrak{p}_{\theta}^{+}

•

The flag manifold
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}^{+}:=G/P_{\theta}^{+}

•

Analogously
\mathfrak{p}_{\theta^{c}}^{-},P_{\theta^{c}}^{-}
, and
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}^{-}

•

The Levi subgroup and subalgebra

L_{\theta}=P^{+}_{\theta}\cap P^{-}_{\theta}\quad\mathfrak{l}_{\theta}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{\theta}\cap\mathfrak{p}^{-}_{\theta}=\mathfrak{g}_{0}\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Phi_{\theta^{c}}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}.

•

The maximal compact of the Levi:

M_{\theta}:=K\cap L_{\theta}.

For example, when
\theta=\Delta
the parabolic
P_{\theta}^{+}
is the minimal one and the flag manifold
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}^{+}
is the maximal one, i.e. it parametrizes full flags.

4.1.5.
Partial order

Continue with the fixed (nonempty) set
\theta\subset\Delta
of simple roots.
Any other root
\lambda
can be expressed as

\lambda=\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta}n_{\alpha}(\lambda)\alpha\quad\text{ with }n_{\alpha}(\lambda)\in\mathbb{Z}.

Define a partial order on the weight lattice by the requirement:

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}\succ_{\theta}\lambda\text{ if and only if: }\forall\alpha\in\Delta\text{ we have }

\displaystyle n_{\alpha}(\lambda^{\prime})\geq n_{\alpha}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\text{ and }\exists\alpha\in\theta\text{ such that }

\displaystyle n_{\alpha}(\lambda^{\prime})>n_{\alpha}(\lambda).

Note that the definition of the partial order naturally extends to any real
\lambda
.

4.1.6.
Polar or
KAK
decomposition, Cartan projection

Recall that any
g\in G
can be expressed as

g=k_{-}(g)e^{\mu(g)}k_{+}(g)\quad k_{\pm}(g)\in K\text{ and }\mu(g)\in\mathfrak{a}^{+}.

While
\mu(g)
is unique, the elements
k_{\pm}(g)
are well-defined only up to a choice of element
m\in K
which centralizes
e^{\mu(g)}
, by changing the elements to
k_{-}(g)m
and
m^{-1}k_{+}(g)
.
Clearly any element in
Z_{K}(\mathfrak{a})
can be used as
m
, but for some
e^{\mu(g)}
the centralizer can be larger.
The map

(4.1.7)

\displaystyle\mu\colon G\to\mathfrak{a}^{+}

is called the
Cartan projection
of
G
.

4.1.8 Definition

(
\theta
-divergence)
.

Let
\theta\subset\Delta
be a subset of the simple roots.

(i)

A sequence
v_{i}\in\mathfrak{a}^{+}
is called
\theta
-divergent
if

\alpha(v_{i})\to+\infty\quad\forall\alpha\in\theta.

(ii)

A sequence
\gamma_{i}\in G
is
\theta
-divergent
if
\mu(\gamma_{i})
is.

(iii)

A subset
\Gamma\subset G
is
\theta
-divergent
if every sequence in
\Gamma
that leaves every compact set in
G
is
\theta
-divergent.

We will typically be interested in the situation when
\Gamma\subset G
is a discrete subgroup.

4.1.9.
Opposition involution

Recall (see
[
Bum13
, Thm. 29.1]
and discussion afterwards) that there exists
w_{0}\in K
, whose adjoint action preserves
\mathfrak{a}
and has the following property.
Given any
g\in G
,
w_{0}
relates the
KAK
decompositions of
g
and
g^{-1}
by

g=k_{-}e^{\mu}k_{+}\Rightarrow g^{-1}=k_{+}^{-1}e^{-\mu}k_{-}^{-1}=(k_{+}^{-1}w_{0}^{-1})e^{\Ad_{w_{0}}(-\mu)}(w_{0}k_{-}^{-1})

The element
w_{0}
is well-defined only up to the left and right action of
Z_{K}(\mathfrak{a})
.

Concretely,
\Ad_{w_{0}}(-\mu)\in\mathfrak{a}^{+}
if
\mu\in\mathfrak{a}^{+}
.
Therefore
w_{0}
gives the action of the longest element in the Weyl group and the map
\mu\mapsto\Ad_{w_{0}}(-\mu)
is called the
opposition involution
on
\mathfrak{a}^{+}
and
\mathfrak{a}
.

4.1.10 Example

(The root system of
\Sp_{2g}
)
.

Let
V
be a
2g
-dimensional real vector space with a non-degenerate symplectic form and set
G=\Sp(V),\mathfrak{g}=\Lie G
.
Choose an identification of
V
with
\mathbb{R}^{2g}
such that the symplectic form is
\begin{bmatrix}0&{\bm{1}}_{g}\\
-{\bm{1}}_{g}&0\end{bmatrix}
and choose the maximal compact subgroup

K=\left\{\begin{bmatrix}A&-B\\
B&A\end{bmatrix}\colon A+\sqrt{-1}B\in U(g)\right\}

to be the unitary group
U(g)=\left\{X\in\Mat_{g\times g}(\mathbb{C})\colon XX^{\dagger}={\bm{1}}_{g}\right\}
.
The Cartan subalgebra can be chosen to consist of diagonal matrices

\mathfrak{a}=\left\{v=\begin{bmatrix}D&0\\
0&-D\\
\end{bmatrix}\colon D=\begin{bmatrix}\mu_{1}(v)&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&\mu_{g}(v)\end{bmatrix}\right\}

Figure 4.1.11.
Left: The root system of
\Sp_{4}
, with its Weyl chamber.
The fundamental weights are
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
.
Middle: Weights for the standard representation.
Right: Weights for the second exterior power of the standard representation.

The roots are then

\Phi=\left\{\pm 2\mu_{i}\colon 1\leq i\leq g\right\}\cup\left\{\pm\mu_{i}\pm\mu_{j}\colon 1\leq i<j\leq g\right\}

and we can choose the simple roots

(4.1.12)

\displaystyle\Delta=\left\{\alpha_{1}:=\mu_{1}-\mu_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{g-1}=\mu_{g-1}-\mu_{g},\alpha_{g}=2\mu_{g}\right\}

so that the positive chamber is

\mathfrak{a}^{+}=\left\{v\in\mathfrak{a}\colon\mu_{1}(v)\geq\mu_{2}(v)\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{g}(v)\geq 0\right\}

Note that the opposition involution is trivial (equal to the identity) for the symplectic group.
Equivalently, the longest element
w_{0}
in the Weyl group is
-{\bm{1}}
.

4.1.13.
Notation and conventions for surfaces

We now set some notation and conventions on surfaces that we follow in the text.
Throughout
X
denotes a complete, finite volume, hyperbolic real
2
-dimensional orbifold.
Fix a basepoint
x_{0}\in X
that is not in the orbifold part.
This gives a fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X,x_{0})
and a universal cover
\widetilde{X}
.
We will frequently write
\pi_{1}(X)
instead of
\pi_{1}(X,x_{0})
.
There is a canonical lift of the basepoint to the universal cover, which by abuse of notation will still be denoted
x_{0}
.
Fix an isometry of
\widetilde{X}
with the hyperbolic plane
\mathbb{H}
in the upper half-plane model.

Most differential-geometric and coarse-geometric constructions are insensitive to passing to a finite-index subgroup.
In those situations, we can and do pass to a finite cover and assume that
X
has no orbifold points and is orientable.

4.1.14.
Standard cusp neighborhood

Consider the region in the upper half-plane
\{\operatorname{Im}\uptau>A\}
and the quotient by
\uptau\mapsto\uptau+1
.
Parts of the surface
X
which are isometric to such quotients will be called
standard cusp neighborhoods
.
As
A\to+\infty
the size of the standard cusp neighborhood shrinks.

In examples where the orbifold fundamental group is generated by reflections as in
Section 3
, standard cusp neighborhoods means quotients of
\{\operatorname{Im}\uptau>A\}
by the two reflections
\uptau\mapsto-\overline{\uptau}
and
\uptau\mapsto-\overline{\uptau}+1
.

4.1.15.
Compact part of a surface

For each puncture of
X
fix a standard cusp neighborhood and denote their union by
X_{O}
, an open set.
Denote the complement by
X_{K}
and call it the
compact part
of
X
.
We require that the cusp neighborhoods are disjoint and small enough that the inclusion
X_{K}\hookrightarrow X
induces an isomorphism of fundamental groups.

4.1.16.
Geometry in the universal cover

Let
\partial\widetilde{X}
denote the visual boundary of the universal cover.
It can be viewed as the set of equivalence classes of geodesic rays, with two rays equivalent when they are a bounded distance apart.
Equivalently, for a fixed basepoint it can be viewed as the set geodesic rays starting at the basepoint.

4.1.17 Definition

(Opposite point)
.

For
x\in\widetilde{X}
and
p\in\partial\widetilde{X}
, define
op_{x}(p)\in\partial\widetilde{X}
to be the boundary point obtained by extending the geodesic ray from
x
to
p
in the opposite direction.

4.1.18.
Two-point boundary

Consider the set of (ordered) distinct points on the boundary:

\partial\widetilde{X}^{(2)}:=\partial\widetilde{X}\times\partial\widetilde{X}\setminus\text{diag}(\partial\widetilde{X}).

This is a noncompact space, but for every
x\in X
, the embedding

p\mapsto(p,op_{x}(p))\quad\partial\widetilde{X}\hookrightarrow\partial\widetilde{X}^{(2)}

has compact image.

4.1.19.
Fundamental domain

Fix a fundamental domain on
\widetilde{X}
with piecewise geodesic boundary, say a Dirichlet domain for
x_{0}
.
Let
\widetilde{X}_{K}
denote the lift of the compact part to the fundamental domain and
\widetilde{X}_{O}
the lift of the complement, also inside the fundamental domain.

4.1.20.
Cusps, peripheral subgroups

Every cusp of
X
determines a
\pi_{1}(X)
-invariant collection of points in
\partial\widetilde{X}
.
We call these
parabolic points
and the stabilizer in
\pi_{1}(X)
of a parabolic point will be called a
peripheral subgroup
.
For instance, in the examples of
Section 3
peripheral subgroups are isomorphic to
(\mathbb{Z}/2)*(\mathbb{Z}/2)
and generated by two reflections.
A
parabolic subgroup
will refer to a subgroup of a peripheral subgroup that is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
.

4.1.21.
Unit tangent bundle and geodesic flow

Let
T^{1}X,T^{1}\widetilde{X}
denote the unit tangent bundles of
X
and its universal cover.
After the identification of
\widetilde{X}
with
\mathbb{H}
, we have a identifications

T^{1}\widetilde{X}=\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{R})\text{ and }T^{1}X=\left.\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{$\pi_{1}(X)$}\middle\backslash\raisebox{1.00006pt}{$\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{R})$}\right..

The geodesic flow
g_{t}
is given by the right action of the matrix

g_{t}:=\begin{bmatrix}e^{t}&0\\
0&e^{-t}\end{bmatrix}

where we identify the matrix and the flow it induces when convenient.
We will also use the identification

(4.1.22)

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}\times\partial\widetilde{X}\tilde{\longrightarrow}T^{1}\widetilde{X}

which takes
(x,p)
to the tangent vector at
x
whose geodesic lands at
p
.

4.1.23.
Norm on fundamental group

Because we fixed an underlying complete hyperbolic metric and so a Fuchsian representation
\rho_{F}\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we can put a norm on the fundamental group via

\left\|\gamma\right\|:=\left\|\rho_{F}(\gamma)\right\|\text{ where the last expression is the matrix norm.}

Any other Fuchsian representation would give comparable norms for our later purposes.
Note also that if
X
were compact, it would suffice to use the word length to get a comparable quantity.
In geometric terms, we could have equivalently used
e^{\dist(x_{0},\gamma x_{0})}
, where
x_{0}
is the fixed basepoint in
\widetilde{X}\cong\mathbb{H}^{2}
, or rather
\cosh
of the distance.

4.1.24.
Representations and flat bundles

Given a representation
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to\GL(V)
denote by
\mathbb{V}_{\rho}\to X
the associated local system.
It is constructed as a quotient

\mathbb{V}_{\rho}=\left.\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{$\pi_{1}(X)$}\middle\backslash\raisebox{1.00006pt}{$\widetilde{X}\times V$}\right.\text{ with }\gamma\cdot(x,v)=(\gamma x,\rho(\gamma)v).

We will abuse notation and write
\mathbb{V}_{\rho}\to T^{1}X
for the same local system lifted to the unit tangent bundle.
When convenient, the subscript
\rho
in
\mathbb{V}_{\rho}
will be omitted.
The fiber of
\mathbb{V}
over
x\in X
, resp.
(x,p)\in T^{1}X
, will be denoted
\mathbb{V}(x)
resp.
\mathbb{V}(x,p)
.

4.1.25.
Quasi-unipotency at the cusps

All representations
\rho
of
\pi_{1}(X)
are assumed to have the following property.
For every peripheral subgroup, there is a finite-index cyclic subgroup generated by
\gamma
and such that
\rho(\gamma)=T^{e}\cdot T^{u}
, where
T^{e}
and
T^{u}
commute,
T^{e}
is an elliptic matrix (i.e. contained in a compact group) and
T^{u}
is a unipotent matrix.

4.1.26.
Metrics on flat bundles

For a local system
\mathbb{V}_{\rho}
coming from a representation, a metric on it is a choice of positive definite inner product on each fiber, that varies with appropriately specified regularity (continuous, smooth, real-analytic) on the point on the base.
Every metric on a vector bundle is assumed at least continuous, which suffices for most arguments; the Hodge metrics, to which the conclusions will be applied, are real-analytic.
We will only consider metrics that depend on the basepoint in
X
, not
T^{1}X
.

Fix now a continuous metric on
\mathbb{V}_{\rho}
, such that
h(x)
determines a metric on the vector space
\mathbb{V}_{\rho}(x)
.
Lifting the discussion to the universal cover
\widetilde{X}
, this gives a
\pi_{1}(X)
-equivariant map

(4.1.27)

\displaystyle h\colon\widetilde{X}\to\GL(V)/K(x_{0})

where
K(x_{0})
is the compact group associated to the metric at the basepoint
x_{0}
.
Using the trivialization of
\mathbb{V}_{\rho}
over
\widetilde{X}
as
X\times V
, the norm of a vector
v\in V
at
x\in\widetilde{X}
is given by

(4.1.28)

\displaystyle\left\|v\right\|_{x}:=\left\|h(x)^{-1}v\right\|_{x_{0}}

where any
K(x_{0})
-coset representative of
h(x)
can be used.
With this convention, the norm function is left
\Gamma
-invariant, as it should be:
\left\|\rho(\gamma)v\right\|_{\gamma x}=\left\|v\right\|_{x}
since
h
is.

4.2.
Dominated cocycles and representations

The notion of domination is a classical one in dynamical systems, generalizing that of hyperbolicity.
In this section we develop it further, allowing for a poset indexing the dominating bundles.
We refer to Bochi–Gourmelon
[
BG09
]
and Bochi–Potrie–Sambarino
[
BPS19
]
for more on classical dominated representations and their relation to Anosov representations.

4.2.1.
Setup

For the next definition, take
(M,g_{t})
to be a continuous
1
-parameter flow on a topological space; we will very soon specialize to
M=T^{1}X
as above.
If
M
is a metric space, we will say the flow is Lipschitz if
d(g_{t}x,g_{t}y)\leq Ce^{|t|L}d(x,y)
for any
x,y\in M
and some fixed constants
C,L>0
.

A cocycle
E\to M
over
g_{t}
is a vector bundle with a lift of the action of
g_{t}
to
E
by fiberwise linear transformations.
Cocycles will be equipped with metrics, and if there is an underlying measure the Oseledets theorem applies (see
[
Fil17
]
for more background).
Most frequently the norm of
g_{t}
acting on the fiber of
E
is pointwise bounded by
e^{|t|L}
for some
L>0
.
We will write the action of
g_{t}
on the right.

4.2.2 Definition

(Dominated cocycle)
.

Let
\Theta
be a partially ordered set (poset), with order relation
\succ
.
A cocycle
E\to(M,g_{t})
has a (weak)
\Theta
-dominated splitting, or simply a
(weak) dominated splitting
, if there exists a decomposition

E=\bigoplus_{A\in\Theta}E_{A}\text{ into continuous subcocycles}

and for any compact set
K\subset M
there exist
C=C(K),\varepsilon=\varepsilon(K)>0
, such that for any
A_{1}\succ A_{2}
and
v_{i}\in E_{A_{i}}(m)\setminus 0
, with
m\in K
, we have that

(4.2.3)

\displaystyle C\cdot\frac{\left\|v_{1}g_{t}\right\|}{\left\|v_{1}\right\|}\geq e^{\varepsilon t}\cdot\frac{\left\|v_{2}g_{t}\right\|}{\left\|v_{2}\right\|}\quad\forall t\geq 0.

4.2.4.
Domination in the compact part

In the above definition, if
M
is compact then we take
K=M
from the start.
Suppose now that
M
is not compact.
Then we will say that the splitting of the cocycle is
dominated in the compact part
if, with the notation as in
§4.2.1
the condition from
Eqn. (4.2.3)
holds for all
t\geq 0

and such that
mg_{t}\in K
.

As we shall see in
Theorem A.1.1
, in the case of interest to us, namely
M=T^{1}X
, the condition of being dominated in the compact part implies being dominated in general, as long as the metric is adapted in the sense of
§A.2.1
.

4.2.5 Definition

(Total domination)
.

Let
\Theta
be a poset.
A subset
S\subset\Theta
is called
totally dominated
if, setting
S^{\prime}:=\Theta\setminus S
, it holds that
s^{\prime}\succ s
for any
s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime},s\in S
.
Similarly,
S^{\prime}\subset\Theta
is called
totally dominating
if
S:=\Theta\setminus S^{\prime}
is totally dominated.
Given a
\Theta
-dominated cocycle
E
, set
E_{S}:=\oplus_{A\in S}E_{A}
, and call
E_{S}
totally dominating, or dominated, if
S
is.

An element
X\in\Theta
will be called a
top
if
\{X\}
is totally dominating, and a
bottom
if it is totally dominated.
A top and a bottom of
\Theta
are unique if they exist.
If there exists at least one totally dominating subcocycle, we will omit the adjective “weak” and just say “dominated splitting”.

The reader can consult
§4.3.13
and
§4.3.13
for some examples illustrating the non-trivial poset structure that can arise among dominating bundles.

4.2.6 Remark

(On total domination)
.

In general, good properties of the subcocycles such as Hölder continuity, stability under perturbations, etc. will only hold for totally dominating or totally dominated ones.
Indeed, a totally dominated subcocycle
E_{S}
has a unique totally dominating complement
E_{S^{\prime}}
, and then we are in the classical setting of domination.
We will say that
E=E_{S^{\prime}}\oplus E_{S}
is a dominated splitting.

Finally, let us remark that the dominated cocycles and posets that arise from Anosov representations have additional symmetries, coming from duality and time reversal, see for instance
§4.3.13
below.

4.2.7.
Domination and duality

Recall that to any cocycle
E
we can associate the dual cocycle
E^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}
, of dual vector spaces, with dynamics defined by

(\xi\cdot g_{t})(v):=\xi(v\cdot g_{-t})\quad\forall\xi\in E^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}(x),v\in E(xg_{t}).

When the cocycle comes from a representation of the fundamental group of
M
, this operation corresponds to taking the dual, or contragredient, representation.
Note that if
E
is
\Theta
-dominated, then
E^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}
is
\Theta^{op}
-dominated, where
\Theta^{op}
is the poset with order relation reversed.

4.2.8.
Domination and time reversal

Suppose now that we consider instead of the flow
g_{t}
the time-reversed flow
\tilde{g}_{t}:=g_{-t}
, but keep the cocycle the same.
Then the poset
\widetilde{\Theta}
indexing the domination bundles becomes
\Theta^{op}
.

4.2.9.
Subbundles on
T^{1}X
invariant under the geodesic flow

Suppose that
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to\GL(V)
is a linear representation and
\mathbb{V}_{\rho}
is the associated local system over
X
or
T^{1}X
.
Recall that the unit tangent bundle
T^{1}\widetilde{X}
can be identified with
\partial\widetilde{X}^{(2)}\times\mathbb{R}
such that the action of the geodesic flow is on the
\mathbb{R}
-factor only, by translation.
A
g_{t}
-invariant decomposition of
\mathbb{V}_{\rho}
is then the same as a
\pi_{1}(X)
-equivariant map
\partial\widetilde{X}^{2}\to\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{d}}(V)
where
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{d}}(V)
is the space of decompositions
V=\oplus V_{d_{i}}
and
\mathbf{d}=\{d_{i}\}
.
Note that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{d}}(V)\cong\GL(V)/\prod\GL_{d_{i}}(\mathbb{R})
.

A bit more generally, one can replace
\GL(V)
by a reductive group, or semisimple, group
G
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{d}}(V)
by
G/L_{\theta}
, where
L_{\theta}
is a Levi subgroup of a parabolic group, see
§4.3.6
below.

4.3.
log-Anosov representations

4.3.1.
Setup

We will freely use the notation introduced in
§4.1
, in particular
G
is a real semisimple Lie group,
K
a maximal compact,
\mathfrak{a}
a Cartan subalgebra, and
\theta\subseteq\Delta
a fixed nonempty subset of the simple roots.

4.3.2 Definition

(log-Anosov representation)
.

A representation
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to G
is
\theta
-log-Anosov
if there exist
C,\varepsilon>0
such that

\alpha\Big(\mu(\rho(\gamma))\Big)\geq\varepsilon\cdot\log\left\|\gamma\right\|-C\quad\forall\gamma\in\pi_{1}(X),\forall\alpha\in\theta

where
\mu\colon G\to{\mathfrak{a}^{+}}
is the Cartan projection and
\left\|\gamma\right\|
is the norm introduced in
§4.1.23
using a(ny) Fuchsian representation.

4.3.3.
Basic properties

Since the geodesic flow on
T^{1}X
is conjugate to its time reversal, it follows that we can take
\theta
to be invariant under the opposition involution, i.e.
\theta=\theta^{op}
, see
[
GW12
, Lemma 3.18]
.
This can also be deduced from the discussion in
§4.2.8
, as well as the formalism developed below for passing between log-dominated and log-Anosov representations.

The following is a consequence of results of Zhu
[
Zhu19
, Thm. 1.2]
and can also be deduced from the results of Guéritaud, Guichard, Kassel, and Wienhard
[
GGKW17
, Thm. 1.1]
(though it doesn’t apply directly in this setting, the mechanism is the same and can be deduced from Lemma 5.8 of loc.cit.)
Recall that
\theta=\theta^{op}
, so we can speak of the transversality of two
\theta
-parabolics.

4.3.4 Theorem

(Existence of boundary maps)
.

Suppose that
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to G
is
\theta
-log-Anosov.
Then there exists a continuous,
\rho
-equivariant map

\xi\colon\partial\widetilde{X}\to\mathcal{F}_{\theta}^{+}

which is furthermore dynamics-preserving and satisfies transversality.

We will write from now on
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
to refer to
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}^{+}
.
Let us note that
\xi
is Hölder-continuous, although we will not need this fact (see e.g.
[
ABF16
, Thm. A]
for a statement which, when applied with uniform constants, implies the result).

4.3.5.
Transversality and dynamics

In the statement of the above theorem, transversality means that such for two distinct points on the boundary
p_{1}\neq p_{2}
, the two parabolics
\xi(p_{1}),\xi(p_{2})
are transverse.
Being dynamics-preserving means the following.
If
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(X)
corresponds to a closed geodesic with endpoints
p_{\pm}\in\partial\widetilde{X}
, then
\rho(\gamma)
acts on
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
with attracting/repelling fixed points at
\xi(p_{\pm})
, and vice versa for
\gamma^{-1}
.
Note that in general
\rho(\gamma)
will have other fixed points as well, with intermediate (i.e. a mixture of attracting and repelling) behavior, but these are never in the image of
\xi
.

If
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(X)
stabilizes a parabolic point
p
on
\partial\widetilde{X}
(and generates a cyclic subgroup), then
\xi(p)
is a fixed point of the unipotent
\rho(\gamma)
and furthermore the corresponding fixed flag is a subflag of the monodromy weight filtration as defined in
§A.1.7
.
Note that
[
GGKW17
]
does not directly address the unipotent case, but
[
Zhu19
, Def. 7.1]
does.
The assertions in the parabolic case also follow from
§A.3
below.

4.3.6.
Two and three-point boundary maps

Because distinct parabolics in the image of the boundary map
\xi
from
Theorem 4.3.4
are transverse, and because
\theta=\theta^{op}
by assumption, we obtain an induced map from the two-point boundary to conjugacy classes of Levi subgroups:

\displaystyle\xi^{(2)}

\displaystyle\colon\partial\widetilde{X}^{(2)}\to G/L_{\theta}

\displaystyle\xi^{(2)}

\displaystyle(p_{1},p_{2}):=\xi(p_{1})\cap\xi(p_{2})

which is
\pi_{1}(X)
-equivariant.

Recall that
\partial\widetilde{X}^{(2)}
is identified with the quotient of
T^{1}\widetilde{X}
by the geodesic flow.
Consider the principal (right)
G
-bundle
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\to T^{1}\widetilde{X}
, which descends to
\mathcal{G}\to T^{1}X
.
Then the data of the map
\xi^{(2)}
is the same as an equivariant section of
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}/L_{\theta}
, which therefore descends to a
g_{t}
-invariant section
\sigma_{2}
of
\mathcal{G}/L_{\theta}\to T^{1}X
.

Recall that
M_{\theta}:=K\cap L_{\theta}
is a maximal compact in
L_{\theta}
, therefore
L_{\theta}/M_{\theta}
is a contractible symmetric space.
Therefore, we can lift the section
\sigma
to a continuous section
\sigma_{3}
of
\mathcal{G}/M_{\theta}\to T^{1}X
, since the fibers of
\mathcal{G}/M_{\theta}\to\mathcal{G}/L_{\theta}
are contractible.

Note that we can make an arbitrary choice in the cusp, and then glue it by a partition of unity (using barycenters in the symmetric space) in the compact part.
In the cusp, it is most natural to use the “strictly adapted metrics” from
§A.2.1
, which in particular give homomorphisms
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{R})\to G
compatible with the unipotent, and use the identification between
T^{1}\widetilde{X}
and
\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
However, we will only look at the behavior of
\sigma_{3}
in the preimage of the compact part in
T^{1}\widetilde{X}
, so the choice in the cusp is immaterial for the arguments below.

We can now lift
\sigma_{3}
to a map called
\xi^{(3)}
on the universal cover and obtain a commutative diagram:

(4.3.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{1}\widetilde{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G/M_{\theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial\widetilde{X}^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G/L_{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{(3)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{(2)}}

4.3.8.
Pullback and pushforward of the Anosov condition

It is quite immediate to describe the “pullback” behavior of the Anosov condition, see e.g.
[
GW12
, Prop. 4.1]
(note that our conventions are opposite of loc.cit. in that the roles of
\theta
and
\theta^{c}
are reversed).
Namely, suppose
\phi\colon G_{1}\to G_{2}
is a homomorphism of real semisimple Lie groups and
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to G_{1}
is a representation.
We will put the respective index (
1
or
2
) on the Lie theoretic data associated to each group.
Suppose that
\phi\circ\rho
is
\theta_{2}
-Anosov.
Then
\rho
is
\theta_{1}
-Anosov, where
\alpha\in\theta_{1}
if and only if
\alpha\notin\operatorname{span}_{\alpha_{2}\in\theta_{2}^{c}}\phi^{t}(\alpha_{2})
, where we can arrange the choice of Lie theoretic data such that
\phi
induces a map
\mathfrak{a}_{1}\xrightarrow{\phi}\mathfrak{a}_{2}
and a reverse map
\mathfrak{a}_{2}^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}\xrightarrow{\phi^{t}}\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}
.
This result is immediate for both Anosov, and log-Anosov representations.

The behavior under “pushforward”, i.e. knowing that
\rho
is
\theta_{1}
-Anosov, and concluding something analogous for
\phi\circ\rho
, requires a better control on how the positive Weyl chambers are mapped, see
[
GW12
, Prop. 4.6]
.
Instead of developing that aspect, we will see how the language of weakly dominated bundles allows us to get analogous information, even in situations where the Anosov conclusion might not hold on
G_{2}
, see e.g.
§4.3.13
and the example in
[
GW12
, §4.3]
.

4.3.9.
Partial order on Levi-irreducible pieces

Recall that
L_{\theta}
denotes a Levi subgroup of
P_{\theta}^{+}
, defined in
§4.1.4
, and
\mathfrak{l}_{\theta}
is its Lie algebra.
Let
V
be a representation of
G
(irreducibility is not needed here, but can be assumed).
Decompose
V
according to irreducible
L_{\theta}
, or
\mathfrak{l}_{\theta}
, representations:

V=\oplus V^{i,\theta}

Each piece in turn decomposes further into weight subspaces for the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}
, with corresponding nonempty root subspaces
V_{\alpha}^{i,\theta}
with
\alpha\in S^{i,\theta}\subset\mathfrak{a}^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}
.
Coarsening the partial order
\succ_{\theta}
from
§4.1.5
, define a partial order on the set of
L_{\theta}
-subrepresentations of
V
by:

\displaystyle V^{i,\theta}\succ_{\theta}^{V}V^{j,\theta}\text{ if and only if }\alpha\succ_{\theta}\alpha^{\prime}\quad\forall\alpha\in S^{i,\theta},\forall\alpha^{\prime}\in S^{j,\theta}.

In other words, a representation
\theta
-dominates a second one if and only if any of its weights
\theta
-dominates any of the weights in the second one.

Note that this notion of domination is not absolute on
L_{\theta}
-representations, but rather depends on how they occur inside
V
.
Additionally, there could be several isotypic components of
\mathfrak{l}_{\theta}
-representations (see e.g. the proof of
[
Fil16
, Thm. 6.8]
for how to gather those together in an invariant way).
For economy of notation, we will omit the vector space of isotypic components.

4.3.10.
Metrics on cocycles

Continuing with the linear representation
G\xrightarrow{\phi}\SL(V)
, and make the choices such that the maximal compact of
G
maps inside a maximal compact in
\SL(V)
, and similarly for maximally split Cartan algebras.
Let
\mathbb{V}\to T^{1}X
be the cocycle associated to this linear representation.
We will equip
\mathbb{V}
with a metric which depends on the point in
T^{1}X
, not just
X
, using
\xi^{(3)}
.
Indeed the projection
G/M_{\theta}\to G/K
, composed with
\SL(V)/\SO(V)
, yields the needed metric.

Next, recall that a
G
-equivariant bundle on
G/L_{\theta}
is the same as an
L_{\theta}
-representation.
Therefore, the
G
-equivariant vector bundle
\mathcal{V}:=(G/L_{\theta})\times V
has a
G
-equivariant decomposition
\mathcal{V}=\oplus\mathcal{V}^{i,\theta}
according to the decomposition into
L_{\theta}
-irreducibles from
§4.3.9
.
By
§4.3.6
, the
\theta
-log-Anosov representation admits a
\pi_{1}(X)
-equivariant map

\xi^{(2)}\colon\partial\widetilde{X}^{(2)}\to G/L_{\theta}

such that, applying the construction of
§4.2.9
, we obtain the
g_{t}
-invariant subbundles on
T^{1}X
.
These will be denoted
\mathbb{V}^{i,\theta}
and with indexing in bijection with the representations
V^{i,\theta}
.

4.3.11 Theorem

(Domination in log-Anosov representations)
.

Suppose that
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to G
is
\theta
-log-dominated.
Let
\phi\colon G\to\GL(V)
be a representation of
V
.
Define
\Theta(\phi)
to be the set of representations of
\mathfrak{l}_{\theta}
occurring in
V
, with the order relation as defined
§4.3.9
.

Then
\phi\circ\rho
is
\Theta(\phi)
-log-dominated in the compact part, with bundles induced from the boundary map
\xi^{(3)}
from
Eqn. (4.3.7)
, and the
G
-equivariant bundles on
G/L_{\theta}
via the construction of
§4.2.9
.

As will be proved in
Theorem A.1.1
, if the metric is adapted, in the sense of
§A.2.1
, the domination condition holds for all times
t\geq 0
and all starting points in a compact set.

Proof.

We will use the map
\xi^{(3)}
from
Eqn. (4.3.7)
to check the domination condition, and the metric described in
§4.3.10
.
First, recall that
\widetilde{X}_{K}\subset\widetilde{X}
denotes a fundamental domain for the compact part
X_{K}\subset X
.
We will verify the domination condition along geodesics of the form
(p,op_{x}(p))\in\partial\widetilde{X}^{(2)}
, for starting and returning points in the compact part of
X
, and where
x\in\widetilde{X}_{K}
, and our estimates will be uniform for such
x
.

Consider now one such geodesic, say
(p,op_{x}(p))\in\partial\widetilde{X}^{(2)}
, and a point
y
on it, projecting to the compact part of
X
.
Let
u_{x},u_{y}\in T^{1}\widetilde{X}
be the corresponding unit vectors.
By the construction of the map
\xi^{(3)}
, we have that
\xi^{(3)}(u_{x})
and
\xi^{(3)}(u_{y})
, as elements of
G/M_{\theta}
, project to the same point in
G/L_{\theta}
, namely
\xi^{(2)}(p,op_{x}(p))
.
It follows that there exist
g_{x}\in G
and
l=l(u_{x},u_{y})\in L_{\theta}
such that

\xi^{(3)}(u_{x})=g_{x}\cdot M_{\theta}\quad\text{ and }\xi^{(3)}(u_{y})=g_{x}\cdot l\cdot M_{\theta}

and furthermore
g_{x}
(it depends on
u_{x}
, not just
x
) can be picked in a compact set of
G
, depending only on the fixed compact part of
X
, and
\rho
as well as the lift
\xi^{(3)}
.

Recall that the flat vector bundle
\mathbb{V}=T^{1}\widetilde{X}\times V
decomposes into invariant subbundles according to the maps
\xi^{(2)}
, i.e. if
V=\oplus V^{i,\theta}
is the decomposition into representations, then over
u\in T^{1}\widetilde{X}
the decomposition is
\oplus\mathcal{V}^{i,\theta}(u)=\xi^{(2)}(u)\cdot\oplus V^{i,\theta}
.
To compute the norm of a vector
v\in V
at
u
, we need to apply the
\xi^{(3)}
-map, namely
\left\|v\right\|_{u}:=\left\|\xi^{(3)}(u)^{-1}v\right\|_{u_{0}}
, where
u_{0}
is a reference basepoint in
T^{1}\widetilde{X}
, sitting over the reference basepoint
x_{0}\in\widetilde{X}
, and such that
\xi^{(3)}(u_{0})
is the identity coset in
G/M_{\theta}
.

Take now two
L_{\theta}
-subrepresentations
V^{1,\theta}\succ_{\theta}^{V}V^{2,\theta}
, and suppose
v_{i}\in\mathcal{V}^{i,\theta}(u_{x})
are unit vectors.
So
v_{i}=g_{x}v_{i,0}
with
v_{i,0}\in V^{i,\theta}
, and such that
\left\|v_{i}\right\|_{u_{x}}\approx\left\|v_{i,0}\right\|_{u_{0}}
, i.e. the norms are comparable up to uniform constants since
x
belongs to a fixed compact fundamental domain.

We now want to compute the norms of the same vectors, but at
u_{y}
(i.e. their parallel transport to
u_{y}
, which in the trivialization on the universal cover does not change the vectors).
Then by definition we have

\displaystyle\left\|v_{i}\right\|_{u_{y}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\xi^{(3)}(u_{y})^{-1}v_{i}\right\|_{u_{0}}=\left\|\xi^{(3)}(u_{y})^{-1}g_{x}v_{i,0}\right\|_{u_{0}}

\displaystyle=\left\|(g_{x}\cdot l\cdot M_{\theta})^{-1}g_{x}v_{i,0}\right\|_{u_{0}}

\displaystyle=\left\|l\cdot v_{i,0}\right\|_{u_{0}}

where
l=l(x,y)\in L_{\theta}
.
Let us now write out the KAK decomposition of
l
in
L_{\theta}
as

l=m_{-}e^{\mu(l)}m_{+}\quad\text{with }m_{\pm}\in M_{\theta},\mu(l)\in\mathfrak{a}^{+}.

The vector
m_{+}v_{i,0}
can be arbitrary in
V^{i,\theta}
, but its norm is uniformly bounded above and away from
0
by constants that only depend on the initial geometry.
By assumption
V^{1,\theta}\succ_{\theta}^{V}V^{2,\theta}
, so any root of
V^{1,\theta}
strictly
\theta
-dominates any root of
V^{2,\theta}
.
Therefore, to establish that
\left\|v_{1}\right\|_{u_{y}}\geq\tfrac{1}{C}e^{\varepsilon\cdot d(x,y)}\left\|v_{2}\right\|_{u_{y}}
, for some fixed
C,\varepsilon>0
, it suffices to check that there exist constants
c_{1},\varepsilon>0
such that

\alpha(\mu(l))\geq\varepsilon\cdot d(x,y)-c_{1}\quad\forall\alpha\in\theta.

This, however, follows from
§4.3.1
, which implies this bound with
l
replaced by
g\in G
such that
\xi^{3}(u_{y})=g\cdot\xi^{(3)}(u_{x})
, and standard comparison estimates on the Cartan projection such as Fact 2.18(3) in
[
GGKW17
]
.
∎

The following is a consequence of the proof.

4.3.12 Corollary

(Definitely contracting vectors)
.

Consider the bundle

\mathbb{V}^{-}:=\bigoplus_{0\succ_{\theta}^{V}V^{i,\theta}}\mathbb{V}^{i,\theta}

where the summation is over all
L_{\theta}
-representations such that
any
root in
V^{i,\theta}
is
\theta
-dominated by the zero root.

Then for any
v\in\mathbb{V}^{-}(m)
it holds that
\left\|vg_{t}\right\|\to 0
as
t\to+\infty
.

4.3.13.
An alternative proof of
Theorem 4.3.11

It is also possible to deduce the theorem just proved, as well as the stated corollary, using slight modifications of
[
GW12
, Prop. 4.3]
.
Indeed the quoted result considers a decomposition of
V
into a line and a hyperplane and establishes domination there.
The proof applies almost directly, except that one needs to use the quantity

\min_{\forall\beta_{x}\in V^{x,\theta}}(\beta_{i}-\beta_{j})(\mu(u,t))

where
\mu(u,t)
denotes the Cartan projection that is being controlled, and
\beta_{i},\beta_{j}
are arbitrary roots in
V^{i,\theta},V^{j,\theta}
.
The condition
V^{i,\theta}\succ_{\theta}^{V}V^{j,\theta}
implies that
\beta_{i}-\beta_{j}\succ 0
(for the usual order on roots) and furthermore for at least one
\alpha\in\theta
appears with a positive coefficient:
n_{\alpha}(\beta_{i}-\beta_{j})>0
.

4.3.14 Example

(Adjoint representation of
\Sp_{4}
)
.

We freely use the notation introduced in
§4.1.9
.
The two simple roots are
\alpha_{1}=\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}
and
\alpha_{2}=2\mu_{2}
.
Assume that
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to\Sp_{4}(\mathbb{R})
is
\theta:=\{\alpha_{1}\}
-log-dominated, which is the example of interest in view of
Theorem 5.1.4
below.

The adjoint representation corresponds to the dominant weight
2\omega_{1}=2\mu_{1}
.
The associated
g_{t}
-cocycle splits into six invariant subbundles, with domination relation as depicted in
Figure 4.3.15
.
Note that the trivial root has multiplicity two, but it gets divided among two subbundles.

Figure 4.3.15.
Left: the roots of the adjoint representation.
Right: The poset of representations for
\mathfrak{l}_{\alpha_{1}}
.

4.3.16 Example

(Reducible representation of
\SL_{2}\times\SL_{2}
)
.

We take up an example of Guichard–Wienhard
[
GW12
, §4.3]
.
Namely, let
G:=\SL_{2}(\mathbb{R})\times\SL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, and let
\rho_{i}\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to\SL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
be such that
\rho_{1}
is a Fuchsian representation, while
\rho_{2}
is arbitrary (say, with dense image).
Then
\rho:=(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
is
\alpha_{1}
-Anosov, where
\alpha_{1}
is the simple root of the first factor.
If we take the (reducible) representation which is the direct sum of the standard representations of each factor of
G
, then we obtain a weakly dominated representation.
The poset has three bundles: two
1
-dimensional bundles, one of which dominates the other, coming from the first factor, and a
2
-dimensional bundle which has no domination relation with others coming from the second factor.

4.3.17 Example

(Reducible representation of
\SL_{2}\times\SL_{2}
, bis)
.

We continue with the example
§4.3.13
above, but assume that one of the
\SL_{2}
-factors corresponds to a uniformizing Fuchsian representation, with entries in a ring of integers in a number field, while the second factor is the Galois-conjugate local system which also admits a variation of Hodge structure (of weight
1
).
This situation occurs in Teichmüller dynamics, see for instance
[
McM21
]
or
[
M0̈6
]
, as well as
[
Fil16
]
where the situation beyond
\SL_{2}
is considered.

In this case, the Schwarz lemma implies that the expansion in the first factor
dominates
that in the second factor.
Therefore, the poset structure of the bundles is such that the
1
-dimensional expanding subspace in the first factor dominates the second factor, which in turn dominates the contracting subspace in the first factor.

4.3.18.
Stable filtration

The dominated decomposition constructed in
Theorem 4.3.11
only needs to
\rho
-equivariant map
\xi^{(2)}
defined on
\partial\widetilde{X}^{(2)}
.
This, in turn, is constructed from the boundary map

\xi\colon\partial\widetilde{X}\to\mathcal{F}_{\theta}=G/P_{\theta}

from
Theorem 4.3.4
.
Over
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
, we have
G
-equivariant bundles corresponding to
P_{\theta}
-representations.
Therefore, for any
G
-representation
V
, we can consider the family of
P_{\theta}
-subrepresentations of
V
.
Pulling back to
T^{1}\widetilde{X}
via the boundary map
\xi
, and passing to the finite volume quotient, gives subbundles of
\mathbb{V}
which are invariant not only under the geodesic flow
g_{t}
, but also the unipotent flow
u_{s}=\begin{bmatrix}1&s\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
since
T^{1}\widetilde{X}\cong\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, and the quotient by the parabolic group generated by
g_{t},u_{s}
is
\partial\widetilde{X}
.

Note that any
P_{\theta}
-invariant representation is a direct sum of irreducible
L_{\theta}
-subrepresentations, so the new bundles are direct sums of the bundles arising in the dominated decomposition.
Note that in general, the resulting bundles, which we shall call the “stable filtration”, do not constitute a filtration in the sense that given two distinct ones, it need not be the case that one is strictly contained in the other (see for instance
§4.3.13
).

4.3.19.
Dangerous turn on terminology

We would like to warn the reader about the following inevitable clash of terminology from dynamics and GIT.
In dynamics, it is typical to call a vector, subbundle, etc. “stable” if it decays exponentially fast into the future (or a bit more generally, if the future behavior of the object is atypical).
In GIT (see
§4.4
below) the term “stable” is by contrast used for vectors that grow (exponentially) into the “future”, for all possibly futures (so the behavior of vectors is typical).
We hope that this clash of terminology will not confuse the reader and at any point in the arguments, it will be apparent which context we’re in.

4.4.
Stability and the numerical criterion

The key notion introduced here is that of a “stable point” for a closed group
\Gamma\subset\SL(V)
acting on
\mathbb{P}(V)
.
It allows us to develop the standard proper discontinuity criteria in an economical way.
To streamline the arguments, we consider the limit set of
\Gamma
using the KAK decomposition and view it
inside
the maximal compact
K
.
This has the advantage that the limit set “acts” on various spaces on which
G
or
K
act.
This is readily translated to the usual limit sets via the equalities
K/M=KAN/MAN=G/P
.

4.4.1.
Setup

Let
\Gamma\subset G
be a closed subgroup.
We introduce a notion of limit set for
\Gamma
which is convenient for our later purposes.
Many variations exist, see e.g.
[
Ben97
]
.
We then relate this notion of limit set to a criterion for proper discontinuity of the
\Gamma
-action.

Recall that
\mu\colon G\to\mathfrak{a}^{+}
denotes the Cartan projection.
Denote by
\mathbb{P}\mathfrak{a}^{+}
the set of rays in the Weyl chamber, its elements will be denoted by
[\mu]
and a nonzero lift to
\mathfrak{a}^{+}
will be denoted
\mu
.
Recall also that any
g\in G
has a KAK decomposition
g=k_{-}(g)e^{\mu(g)}k_{+}(g)
.

4.4.2 Definition

(Limit data)
.

The limit data
\mathcal{L}(\Gamma)
of
\Gamma
is the set of accumulation points in
K\times K\times\mathbb{P}\mathfrak{a}^{+}
of sequences

(k_{-}(\gamma_{i}),k_{+}(\gamma_{i}),[\mu(\gamma_{i})])\text{ where }\gamma_{i}\in\Gamma_{i}\text{ is s.t. }\left\|\mu(\gamma_{i})\right\|\to+\infty.

Denote also by
\mathcal{L}_{+}(\Gamma)
the projection to
K\times\mathfrak{a}^{+}
along the first factor of
\mathcal{L}(\Gamma)
.
A
limit ray
is a one-parameter semigroup of the form
k_{+}^{-1}e^{\mu t}k_{+}
for
t\geq 0
, where
(k_{+},[\mu])\in\mathcal{L}_{+}(\Gamma)
.

4.4.3 Remark

(Basics on limit data)
.

It is clear from the definition that
\mathcal{L}(\Gamma)
is a closed set, hence compact because the ambient space is compact (recall that
\mathfrak{a}^{+}
is the closed Weyl chamber).
The KAK decomposition is not unique in general, and we allow in the definition all possible representations.
In particular, the limit data is invariant under the action of
M
by
(k_{-},k_{+},[\mu])\mapsto(k_{-}m^{-1},mk_{+},[\mu])
.

Since
\Gamma
is a group, we can take inverses and this provides an extra symmetry of the limit data.
Namely, let
w_{0}\in K
be an element whose adjoint action gives on
\mathfrak{a}
the action of the longest element of the Weyl group, so that

\displaystyle\left[k_{-}(\gamma)e^{\mu(\gamma)}k_{+}(\gamma)\right]^{-1}

\displaystyle=k_{+}(\gamma)^{-1}e^{-\mu(\gamma)}k_{-}(\gamma)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left[k_{+}(\gamma)^{-1}w_{0}^{-1}\right]\cdot\left[e^{\mu(\gamma)^{op}}\right]\cdot\left[w_{0}k_{-}(\gamma)^{-1}\right]

where
\mu^{op}:=Ad_{w_{0}}(-\mu)
.
It follows that the limit data is symmetric under

(4.4.4)

\displaystyle(k_{-},k_{+},[\mu])\mapsto\left(k_{+}^{-1}w_{0}^{-1},w_{0}k_{-}^{-1},[\mu^{op}]\right).

We now reverse the Hilbert–Mumford criterion to provide a definition of stability:

4.4.5 Definition

(Stable point)
.

Let
V
be a
G
-representation equipped with a
K
-invariant norm.
A point
[v]\in\mathbb{P}(V)
is
\Gamma
-stable, or simply
stable
if
\Gamma
is clear from the context, if for any
(k_{+},[\mu])\in\mathcal{L}_{+}(\Gamma)
, and for any lift
v\in V
, we have along the corresponding limit ray:

\left\|k_{+}^{-1}\cdot e^{\mu\cdot t}\cdot k_{+}v\right\|\xrightarrow{t\to+\infty}+\infty.

The element
k_{+}^{-1}
in the above definition does not affect the norm of the vector.
We explain below in
§4.4.13
that the property of point being stable is independent of the choice of
K
and
\mathfrak{a}
.

4.4.6.
Stable filtration

Fix
[\mu]\in\mathbb{P}\mathfrak{a}^{+}
and a
G
-representation
V
.
The weight space decomposition of
V
for the action of
\mathfrak{a}
induces a coarse decomposition

V=V^{<0}([\mu])\oplus V^{0}([\mu])\oplus V^{>0}([\mu])

where the superscript indicates the result of pairing the subspace with any lift of
\mu
to
\mathfrak{a}^{+}
.
We will also write
V^{\leq 0}([\mu])
for the sum of the first two, and
V^{\geq 0}([\mu])
for the sum of the last two.
It is clear that

(4.4.7)

\displaystyle v\notin V^{\leq 0}([\mu])\iff\left\|e^{\mu\cdot t}v\right\|\xrightarrow{t\to+\infty}+\infty.

We also have an upper semicontinuity property of
V^{\leq 0}([\mu])
, namely if
[\mu_{i}]\to[\mu]
in
\mathbb{P}\mathfrak{a}^{+}
and
[v_{i}]\in\mathbb{P}\left(V^{\leq 0}([\mu_{i}])\right)
, with
[v_{i}]\to[v]
, then it holds that
[v]\in\mathbb{P}\left(V^{\leq 0}([\mu])\right)
.
Note incidentally that the spaces
V^{\bullet}([\mu])
can take only finitely many values as
[\mu]
ranges in
\mathbb{P}\mathfrak{a}^{+}
.

Finally, let
w_{0}\in K
be again the longest element in the Weyl group.
Because of the calculation (where
\alpha
is a weight):

\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle=\left\langle w_{0}\alpha,w_{0}\mu\right\rangle=-\left\langle w_{0}\alpha,-w_{0}\mu\right\rangle=-\left\langle w_{0}\alpha,\mu^{op}\right\rangle

it follows that

(4.4.8)

\displaystyle w_{0}\cdot V^{<0}\left([\mu]\right)=V^{>0}\left([\mu^{op}]\right)\text{ and }w_{0}\cdot V^{0}\left([\mu]\right)=V^{0}\left([\mu^{op}]\right).

4.4.9.
Dynamically related points

To establish proper discontinuity of actions, the following notion due to Frances
[
Fra05
, §3.2]
is convenient.
Let
S
be a topological space and
\Gamma\subset\Homeo(S)
a group.
Two points
x,y\in S
are
dynamically related
by a sequence
\left\{\gamma_{i}\right\}\subset\Gamma
if there exists a sequence
x_{i}\to x
such that
\gamma_{i}(x_{i})\to y
.
We will say that two points are
\Gamma
-dynamically related (for
\Gamma\subset\Homeo(S)
closed) if they are related by a sequence contained in
\Gamma
, and which leaves every compact set in
\Gamma
.
Note that being dynamically related is a reflexive relation, since setting
y_{i}:=\gamma_{i}x_{i}\to y
one sees that
\gamma_{i}^{-1}y_{i}=x_{i}\to x
holds.

The main application of the definition of stability is to establish:

4.4.10 Theorem

(Proper discontinuity criterion)
.

Keep the notation from above.

(i)

A point
[v]\in\mathbb{P}(V)
is stable if and only if for any
(k_{+},[\mu])\in\mathcal{L}_{+}(\Gamma)
we have that

k_{+}\cdot[v]\notin V^{\leq 0}([\mu]).

Therefore, the set of stable points in
\mathbb{P}(V)
is open (but possibly empty).

(ii)

Two distinct stable points cannot be dynamically related.

(iii)

If
M\subset\mathbb{P}(V)
is a locally closed
\Gamma
-invariant set, and
M^{s}\subset M
denotes its intersection with the stable points, then
\Gamma
acts properly discontinuously on
M^{s}
when it is equipped with the subspace topology.

Proof.

The criterion of stability in part (i) is just a restatement of
Eqn. (4.4.7)
with the inclusion of a
k_{+}
-term.
To check that the set of stable points is open is equivalent to establishing that its complement is closed.
Inside
K\times\mathbb{P}\mathfrak{a}^{+}\times\mathbb{P}(V)
, consider the set of points of the form
(k,[\mu],[v])
such that
(k,[\mu])\in\mathcal{L}_{+}(\Gamma)
and
k\cdot[v]\in V^{\leq 0}([\mu])
.
This set it closed (and compact) because
\mathcal{L}_{+}(\Gamma)
is, and because of the upper semicontinuity property of the stable filtration discussed in
§4.4.6
.
The complement of the set of stable points is precisely the image of the constructed set under projection to
\mathbb{P}(V)
.

Clearly part (iii), proper discontinuity, follows from (ii).
So assume, by contradiction, that two dynamically related points exist (see
§4.4.9
), take corresponding sequences, and denote by
x_{i},x,y
a choice of lifts to
V
.
Write the KAK decomposition of
\gamma_{i}=k_{-}(\gamma_{i})e^{\mu(\gamma_{i})}k_{+}(\gamma_{i})
and pass to a subsequence such that there is a limit
(k_{-},k_{+},[\mu])\in\mathcal{L}(\Gamma)
.
From the assumed limit properties, it follows that

k_{+}(\gamma_{i})x_{i}\xrightarrow{i\to\infty}k_{+}x\quad\text{ and }\quad k_{-}^{-1}(\gamma_{i})y\xrightarrow{i\to\infty}k_{-}^{-1}y.

Because
x
is stable by assumption, and using the criterion from
Eqn. (4.4.7)
, it follows that
k_{+}x\notin V^{\leq 0}([\mu])
, and so there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
d(k_{+}[x],\mathbb{P}(V^{\leq 0}[\mu]))>\varepsilon
for the induced distance on projective space.
It follows that for sufficiently large
i
, we have that
d(k_{+}(\gamma_{i})x_{i},V^{\leq 0}([\mu_{i}]))>\varepsilon/2
, otherwise we could pass along a subsequence to a limit and contradict the lower bound of
\varepsilon
on
k_{+}[x]
just obtained (again, combined with the upper semicontinuity of
V^{\leq 0}
).

Because
y
is stable, and using the symmetry of
\mathcal{D}(\Gamma)
under taking inverses from
Eqn. (4.4.4)
, it follows that
w_{0}k_{-}^{-1}y\notin V^{\leq 0}([\mu^{op}])
.
Now applying
w_{0}
to both sides and recalling that it is an involution, combined with the formula for its action from
Eqn. (4.4.8)
, it follows that
k_{-}^{-1}y\notin V^{\geq 0}([\mu])
.

From the assumption that
[x],[y]
are dynamically related, and using the KAK decomposition of
\gamma_{i}
, we know that

[z_{i}]:=e^{\mu(\gamma_{i})}k_{+}(\gamma_{i})[x_{i}]\to k_{-}^{-1}[y]\notin V^{\geq 0}([\mu]).

However, we established that there is a uniform
\varepsilon/2>0
lower bound distance between
k_{+}(\gamma_{i})[x_{i}]
and
V^{\leq 0}([\mu_{i}])
.
It follows that the distance between
[z_{i}]
and
V^{>0}([\mu_{i}])
goes to zero, and by the upper semicontinuity of
V^{\geq 0}
, it follows that any of the accumulation points of
[z_{i}]
must be in
V^{\geq 0}([\mu])
.
This however contradicts the fact that
k_{-}^{-1}[y]\notin V^{\geq 0}([\mu])
.
∎

4.4.11.
Semistable points

It is natural to define
[v]\in\mathbb{P}(V)
to be
semistable
if the condition in
§4.4.1
is replaced by:

\liminf\left\|k_{+}^{-1}\cdot e^{\mu\cdot t}\cdot k_{+}v\right\|>0\text{ as }{t\to+\infty}.

Then
Eqn. (4.4.7)
gets replaced by

v\notin V^{<0}([\mu])\iff\left\|e^{\mu\cdot t}v\right\|\text{ stays bounded as }{t\to+\infty}.

The proof of
Theorem 4.4.10
then goes through verbatim, only changing some of the signs
\leq
by
<
to yield that a semistable point
[x]
and a stable point
[y]
cannot be dynamically related (recall that being dynamically related is also a reflexive relation).
Indeed the last part in the proof changes to the assertion that the distance between
[z_{i}]
and
V^{\geq 0}([\mu_{i}])
goes to zero, and so their accumulation points must still be in
V^{\geq 0}[\mu]
, which contradicts the assumption on
[y]
.
Note that strictly semistable points can be dynamically related, even in the example of a single element of
\SL_{3}(\mathbb{R})
with eigenvalues
e^{\mu},1,e^{-\mu}
acting on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

For how to take these notions further in the context of actions on flag manifolds, see
[
KLP18
, §7.4]
.

4.4.12 Remark

(On the numerical criterion)
.

The Hilbert–Mumford numerical criterion
[
MFK94
, Thm 2.1]
is the basis for our definition of stability and semistability.
Let us remark that a crucial role in the proofs is played by the
KAK
(or polar, or Iwasawa) decomposition for a group
G
, where
K=\mathbf{G}(k\mkern-3.0mu\left[\mkern-3.0mu\left[t\right]\mkern-3.0mu\right]\mkern-2.0mu)
and
G=\mathbf{G}(k\mkern-3.0mu\left(\mkern-4.0mu\left(t\right)\mkern-4.0mu\right)\mkern-2.0mu)
for a field
k
, formal variable
t
, and reductive
k
-algebraic group
\mathbf{G}
.
One could replace further
k\mkern-3.0mu\left[\mkern-3.0mu\left[t\right]\mkern-3.0mu\right]\mkern-2.0mu
by a discrete valuation ring and
k\mkern-3.0mu\left(\mkern-4.0mu\left(t\right)\mkern-4.0mu\right)\mkern-2.0mu
by its field of fractions (so, a local field would work).

4.4.13.
Intrinsic aspects of stability

The notion of stable point in
§4.4.1
, and semistable point in
§4.4.11
, appears to depend on a choice of maximal compact
K
and Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}
.
We now clarify that this is not the case, and any other choices would lead to the same notion.

Let
\mathscr{X}
denote the space of maximal compact subgroups of
G
– when we fix one such, say
K
, we get an identification
\mathscr{X}\cong\left.\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{$K$}\middle\backslash\raisebox{1.00006pt}{$G$}\right.
(we will take this left quotient convention for this paragraph only).
The space
\mathscr{X}
carries a unique up to scale
G
-invariant Riemannian metric, which makes it a
\CAT(0)
space.
As such it has a natural visual boundary
\partial_{vis}\mathscr{X}
, and
\mathbb{P}\mathfrak{a}^{+}
is identified with the space of
K
-orbits on
\partial_{vis}\mathscr{X}
, for any choice of maximal compact
K
.

Next, the
1
-parameter semigroups used in
§4.4.1
determine geodesic rays starting at a fixed basepoint of
\mathscr{X}
, obtained as limits of geodesic rays connecting the basepoint with its images under
\Gamma
.
In other words, the semigroups used in
§4.4.1
are those determined by the closure of
\Gamma
in the visual compactification of
\mathscr{X}
.
By the
\CAT(0)
property of
\mathscr{X}
, any other basepoint would lead to geodesic rays which are a bounded distance away from those based at the original basepoint.
This would affect the norms by bounded multiplicative constants, so would not affect the definitions of stability (or semistability).

4.4.14.
Control of the limit data

We now collect some consequences of Anosov-type conditions on the limit data of
\Gamma:=\rho(\pi_{1}(X))
, where
\rho
is
\theta
-(log)-Anosov.
First, it is immediate from the definition
§4.3.1
that the projection of
\mathcal{L}_{+}(\Gamma)
to
\mathfrak{a}^{+}
is contained in the
complement
of the faces
\ker\alpha,\forall\alpha\in\theta
.

Control on the
K
-component comes from
Theorem 4.3.4
and its consequence,
Theorem 4.3.11
.
Freeze
K,\mathfrak{a}^{+}
for the rest of this discussion.
Then recall that we can re-express the flag manifold
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}:=G/P_{\theta}=K/M_{\theta}
and we have the boundary map

(4.4.15)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\xi&\colon\partial\widetilde{X}\to\mathcal{F}_{\theta}=\left.\raisebox{1.00006pt}{$K$}\middle/\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{$M_{\theta}$}\right.\end{split}

Let us also denote by
\pi_{K,\theta}
the composition

\pi_{K,\theta}\colon K\to\left.\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{$M_{\theta}$}\middle\backslash\raisebox{1.00006pt}{$K$}\right.\xrightarrow{g\mapsto g^{-1}}\left.\raisebox{1.00006pt}{$K$}\middle/\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{$M_{\theta}$}\right..

The key consequence of the Anosov property is then:

4.4.16 Theorem

(Control of
K
component)
.

The projection of
\mathcal{L}_{+}(\Gamma)
to
K
is contained in
\pi_{K,\theta}^{-1}(\xi(\partial\widetilde{X}))
.

Denote from now on by
\mathcal{L}K_{+}
the projection of
\mathcal{L}_{+}
to the
K
-component.

Proof.

The assertion is a direct consequence of
[
GGKW17
, Thm. 5.3]
and the discussion in §5.1 of loc.cit. preceding it.
The only changes are that
\Xi_{\theta}
there refers to
k_{-}(g)
in our notation for the
KAK
decomposition of
g
, so there is no need in loc.cit. to apply the inverse map on
G
.
The flag manifold
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
is identified with
G/P_{\theta}
, but
K
acts transitively so it is also
K/M_{\theta}
.
Note also that the “gap summation property” in loc.cit. needs to be replaced by the quantitative divergence of
§4.3.1
and
\log\left\|\gamma\right\|
from
§4.1.23
instead of the word length in the group.
∎

4.5.
Integral vectors in the limit set

4.5.1.
Setup

Let
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to G
be a
\theta
-log-Anosov representation.
Recall that
G
is assumed to be a real semisimple algebraic group, and for this section we assume that
G
has a
\mathbb{Q}
-structure as well, with
\Lambda\subset G
an arithmetic lattice.
Suppose that the image of
\rho
lands in
\Lambda
, and consider a linear representation
G\xrightarrow{\phi}\GL(V)
for which
\Lambda
preserves a lattice
V_{\mathbb{Z}}\subset V_{\mathbb{R}}
.

4.5.2.
Decreasing subbundle

Let
\mathbb{V}_{i,\theta}\subset\mathbb{V}
be the
g_{t}
-invariant subbundles provided by
Theorem 4.3.11
.
We shall consider

\mathbb{V}^{-}:=\bigoplus_{V_{\theta,i}\prec_{\theta}0}\mathbb{V}_{\theta,i}

i.e. all the subbundles for which the weights appearing in them are strictly
\theta
-dominated by the zero weight.
It follows from
§4.3.10
that for any
v\in\mathbb{V}^{-}
, we have that
\left\|vg_{t}\right\|\to 0
as
t\to+\infty
.
In fact
\left\|vg_{t}\right\|\leq Ce^{-\varepsilon t}\left\|v\right\|
for some uniform
C,\varepsilon>0
(assuming that
v
starts in the compact part and we evaluate the norm at times when the flow recurred to the compact part).

We shall consider the associated map

\xi^{-}_{V}\colon\partial\widetilde{X}\to\Gr\left(\rk\mathbb{V}^{-},V\right)

into the Grassmannian parametrizing
\mathbb{V}^{-}
.

4.5.3 Theorem

(Integral vectors must come from cusps)
.

Suppose that
v\in V_{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}
is such that there exists
p\in\partial\widetilde{X}
such that
v\in\xi^{-}_{V}(p)
.

Then
p
is a parabolic boundary point.
Furthermore, let
T
be the corresponding quasi-unipotent monodromy transformation, and let
W_{\bullet}V
be the weight filtration induced by (the logarithm of)
T
, as per
§A.1.7
.
Then
v\in W_{-1}V
.

Proof.

Consider the unit vector
u\in T^{1}\widetilde{X}
over the basepoint
x_{0}
and such that the associated hyperbolic geodesic in
\widetilde{X}
lands at
p
on the boundary.
Denote by
ug_{t}
the geodesic flow at time
t
for
u
.

Suppose that
p
is not a parabolic point, then there exist infinitely many times
t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots
such that the geodesic, projected to
X
, returns to a fixed compact set.
By the construction of
\mathbb{V}^{-}
, the norm
\left\|v\right\|_{ug_{t}}
goes to
0
as
t\to+\infty
, in fact exponentially fast.
On the other hand, given any compact set
K
in
X
, there exists a uniform lower bound
\varepsilon(K)>0
on the norm of any integral vector in
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{Z}}
on
K
.
Since the image of
v
under the monodromy transformations remains integral, this provides a contradiction.

It follows that
p
is indeed a parabolic point, and
\left\|v\right\|_{ug_{t}}
goes to zero as
t\to+\infty
.
By
§A.2.1
, this is equivalent to
v
belonging to the negative part of the weight filtration induced by the monodromy (the exponent
k
in the lemma must be strictly negative, and
\operatorname{Re}\uptau
stays bounded).
∎

4.6.
Minimality of the action on a limit set

4.6.1.
Setup

Let
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to\Sp(V_{\mathbb{R}})
be an
\alpha_{1}
-Anosov representation, where
V_{\mathbb{R}}
is a
4
-dimensional symplectic space.
Set
\theta=\{\alpha_{1}\}
.
By
Theorem 4.3.4
there exists a continuous, equivariant, transverse and dynamics-preserving map

\xi\colon\partial\widetilde{X}\to\mathbb{P}(V_{\mathbb{R}})=\mathcal{F}_{\theta}

Let us call the image curve
\Lambda_{P}:=\xi(\partial\widetilde{X})
, and the image of the representation
\Gamma:=\rho(\pi_{1}(X))
.
Because the action of
\pi_{1}(X)
on
\partial\widetilde{X}
is minimal, it follows that the action of
\Gamma
on
\Lambda_{P}
is minimal.

Recall also that we have a “limit set”
\Lambda_{L}\subset\LGr(V_{\mathbb{R}})
, defined as the set of Lagrangians which contain a line from
\Lambda_{P}
.
Equivalently, for every
\xi(p)\in\Lambda_{P}
we have the photon
\phi(p)\subset\LGr(V_{\mathbb{R}})
, see
§2.3.5
, and
\Lambda_{L}=\cup_{p\in\widetilde{X}}\phi(p)
.

4.6.2.
Assumption on unipotent

We make the following
crucial standing assumption
: there exists at least one cusp in
X
such that the monodromy around it is generated (up to finite index) by a rank
1
unipotent matrix fixing a line and its symplectic orthogonal.
This assumption is satisfied for all hypergeometric examples from
Section 3
, since the monodromy around
1\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
is always a unipotent satisfying the requirement.

We will call any parabolic boundary point with the above property “good”.
By minimality of the action of
\pi_{1}(X)
on
\partial\widetilde{X}
, the set of good parabolic points is dense on the boundary.

The goal of this section is to prove:

4.6.3 Theorem

(Minimality of the action)
.

Under the above assumptions, the action of
\Gamma
on
\Lambda_{L}
is minimal, i.e. every orbit is dense.
Equivalently, any closed
\Gamma
-invariant set is either empty or all of
\Lambda_{L}
.

4.6.4.
Correspondence Principle

Let
P_{\theta^{c}}
be the parabolic such that
\LGr(V_{\mathbb{R}})=G/P_{\theta^{c}}
(here
\theta^{c}:=\Delta\setminus\theta=\{\alpha_{2}\}
is the complement of
\theta
in the set of roots).
It is well-known, and immediate to verify, that there are natural bijections between closed invariant sets, and orbit closures, for the following group actions:

(i)

\Gamma
acting on
\left.\raisebox{1.00006pt}{$G$}\middle/\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{$P_{\theta^{c}}$}\right.
.

(ii)

\Gamma\times P_{\theta^{c}}
acting on
G
by a left-right action of the corresponding factors.

(iii)

P_{\theta^{c}}
acting on the right on
\left.\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{$\Gamma$}\middle\backslash\raisebox{1.00006pt}{$G$}\right.
.

The natural bijections are obtained by passing through the middle action and taking images or preimages.

Recall that the action of
\Gamma
on
\LGr(V_{\mathbb{R}})=G/P_{\theta^{c}}
has a closed invariant set
\Lambda_{L}
, on which the action is minimal by
Theorem 4.6.3
, and the complementary open set
\Omega_{L}
on which the action is proper.
In particular, every orbit of
\Gamma
on
\Omega_{L}
is closed.
We conclude:

4.6.5 Corollary

(Orbit dichotomy in homogeneous space)
.

There exists a closed
P_{\theta^{c}}
-invariant set
\mathcal{R}\subset\left.\raisebox{-1.02228pt}{$\Gamma$}\middle\backslash\raisebox{1.02228pt}{$G$}\right.
, such that the
P_{\theta^{c}}
-orbit of any point in
\mathcal{R}
is dense in
\mathcal{R}
, and the
P_{\theta^{c}}
-orbit of any point outside
\mathcal{R}
is closed in the complement of
\mathcal{R}
.

We can think of
\mathcal{R}
as a “recurrent set” for the
P_{\theta^{c}}
-dynamics.

4.6.6.
Oseledets Lagrangians are dense

We return for a moment to the setting of
§4.6.1
with a finite-volume hyperbolic Riemann surface
X
.
Recall that by the Oseledets Multiplicative Ergodic Theorem (see e.g.
[
Fil17
, 2.2.6]
) there exists a
measurable

\rho
-equivariant map
\xi_{2}\colon\partial\widetilde{X}\to\LGr(V_{\mathbb{R}})
which is given by the second term of the Lyapunov filtration (implicit in this is that the Lyapunov spectrum is simple, which can be established by a proof identical to
[
EM15
, Thm. 4]
).
It follows from
Theorem 4.6.3
that the image of this measurable map is
dense
in
\LGr(V_{\mathbb{R}})
.
One can also view the image as a measurable
\Gamma
-equivariant section of the projection
\Lambda_{L}\to\Lambda_{P}
, which again is dense.
This is reminiscent of Furstenberg’s example of a minimal, but not uniquely ergodic skew product on
\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

4.6.7.
Unipotent dynamics and the fibration

To prove
Theorem 4.6.3
, we need some preliminaries on the dynamics of a rank
1
unipotent as in the standing assumption.

Suppose
l\subset V_{\mathbb{R}}
is a line, and
l^{\perp}
is its symplectic orthogonal, so we have a flag
l\subset l^{\perp}\subset V_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let
T\colon V_{\mathbb{R}}\to V_{\mathbb{R}}
be a nontrivial rank
1
unipotent transformation fixing
l^{\perp}
pointwise and such that
T-{\bm{1}}
maps
V/l^{\perp}
isomorphically onto
l
.
We will identify the photon
\phi(l)
of Lagrangians
L
such that
l\subset L
with
\mathbb{P}(l^{\perp}/l)
, by assigning to
L
the direction
l^{\perp}\cap L
.

Define, more generally, the following “contraction map”

(4.6.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}c_{l}\colon\LGr(V_{\mathbb{R}})\setminus\phi(l)&\to\phi(l)\\
L^{\prime}&\mapsto(l^{\perp}\cap L)\mod l\end{split}

4.6.9 Lemma

(Limits under unipotent iterates)
.

(i)

Suppose that
L^{\prime}\in\LGr(V_{\mathbb{R}})\setminus\phi(l)
is any Lagrangian.
Then as
n\to+\infty
we have

T^{n}L^{\prime}\to c_{l}(L^{\prime})\in\phi(l).

(ii)

Suppose that
l^{\prime}\subset V_{\mathbb{R}}
is another line, such that
\phi(l^{\prime})
and
\phi(l)
are disjoint.
Then the maps

T^{n}|_{\phi(l^{\prime})}\text{ converge to the map }c_{l}|_{\phi(l^{\prime})}\colon\phi(l^{\prime})\to\phi(l).

Furthermore, if we write
V_{\mathbb{R}}=l\oplus\left(l^{\perp}\cap l^{{}^{\prime},\perp}\right)\oplus l^{\prime}
and identify
\phi(l)
and
\phi(l^{\prime})
with
\mathbb{P}\left(l^{\perp}\cap l^{{}^{\prime},\perp}\right)
then
c_{l}
as restricted above is the identity map.

Proof.

The verification is immediate, by computation in a symplectic basis in which
l
is spanned by
e_{1}
,
l^{\perp}
is spanned by
e_{1},e_{2},f_{2}
, and
f_{1}
is such that
T(f_{1})=f_{1}+e_{1}
.
Given
l^{\prime}
as in the second part of the statement, we can choose our basis such that
l^{\prime}
is spanned by
f_{2}
.

Note that since
T
acts as the identity on
l^{\perp}
, under iteration the intersection
T^{n}L^{\prime}\cap l^{\perp}
does not change, while the remaining vector in
L^{\prime}
converges to
L
.
∎

4.6.10.
Notation

For economy of notation, for a point
p\in\partial\widetilde{X}
, we will write
\phi(p)
for the photon
\phi(\xi(p))
.
Similarly, we will write
c_{p}
, instead of
c_{\xi(p)}
, for the associated map defined in
Eqn. (4.6.8)
.
When
p
is a good parabolic boundary point, we will write
T_{p}
for the associated unipotent transformation.

Let from now on
S\subset\Lambda_{L}
be a nonempty, closed,
\Gamma
-invariant set.
Our goal is to show that
S=\Lambda_{L}
, and we will do so by showing that for any good parabolic boundary point
p
, we have that
S\cap\phi(p)=\phi(p)
.
By density of such
p
in
\partial\widetilde{X}
, the result will follow.

4.6.11 Proposition

(Pushing and pulling fibers)
.

Let
p
be a good parabolic boundary point, and
p^{\prime}\in\partial\widetilde{X}\setminus p
a different point.

(i)

We have that

c_{p}\left(S\cap\phi(p^{\prime})\right)\subset S\cap\phi(p).

(ii)

Suppose that
p^{\prime}
is a good parabolic point as well.
Then

c_{p}\left(S\cap\phi(p^{\prime})\right)=S\cap\phi(p).

(iii)

Suppose again that
p^{\prime}
is arbitrary.
Then

c_{p}\left(S\cap\phi(p^{\prime})\right)=S\cap\phi(p).

Clearly the last part implies the previous ones, but we will prove the statements in this order.

Proof.

For (i), because
S
is closed and
T_{p}
-invariant, it follows from
§4.6.7
that any accumulation points of
T_{p}^{n}(S\cap\phi(p^{\prime}))
are contained in
S\cap\phi(p)
, so the result follows.

Part (ii) follows by applying (i) to both parabolic points, going back-and-forth, and using the identification of the photons from
§4.6.7
.

Finally for (iii) we need to prove the reverse inclusion from that in (i).
But take a sequence of good parabolic points
p_{i}
converging to
p^{\prime}
.
Then we have that
\limsup(S\cap\phi(p_{i}))\subset S\cap\phi(p^{\prime})
, where
\limsup
of sets refers to all the accumulation points.
Applying
c_{p}
to the above containment gives the result by (ii).
∎

The following is the key calculation which will give the result.

4.6.12 Proposition

(Fiberwise unipotents)
.

Suppose that
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\in\partial\widetilde{X}
are three distinct good parabolic boundary points.
Set
V_{13}:=l_{p_{1}}^{\perp}\cap l_{p_{3}}^{\perp}
, so that we have a decomposition
V=l_{1}\oplus V_{13}\oplus l_{3}
.
Suppose that
l_{p_{2}}
is
not
contained in
l_{p_{1}}\oplus l_{p_{3}}
, and let
l_{123}\subset V_{13}
be the projection of
l_{p_{2}}
to
V_{13}
, nontrivial by assumption.

Then the composition

\phi(p_{1})\xrightarrow{c_{p_{2}}}\phi(p_{2})\xrightarrow{c_{p_{3}}}\phi(p_{3})\xrightarrow{c_{p_{1}}}\phi(p_{1})

is (the projectivization) of a nontrivial unipotent transformation, with fixed point
l_{123}
when identifying
\phi(p_{1})\cong\mathbb{P}(V_{13})
.

4.6.13.
Finishing the proof

The proof of
§4.6.10
is contained below and is a calculation.
Here we explain why that establishes
Theorem 4.6.3
.
Fix
p_{1},p_{3}
two good, distinct parabolic boundary points.
A nonempty open set
U_{1,3}\subset\partial\widetilde{X}
of
p_{2}
’s will satisfy the assumption of
§4.6.10
, i.e.
l_{p_{2}}
will not be contained in
l_{p_{1}}\oplus l_{p_{3}}
, otherwise this would contradict the Zariski-density of
\Gamma
.
Note also that the image of the projection of
\xi(U_{1,3})
in
\mathbb{P}(V_{1,3})
will contain an open set, for the same reason.

In the open set
U_{1,3}
, a dense collection of good parabolic boundary points is available.

§4.6.10
gives then a family of nontrivial unipotent transformations of
\mathbb{P}(V_{1,3})
